Selective Patch 策略研究

研究目标：在不明显损伤 TF、ECS、完成率及逐任务表现的前提下，提高环境变化后的 PE。报告同时保留 recovery、retention 和更新成本；效率改善不自动等于恢复能力改善。

依据课程 brief 与 MCPEvol 论文，分别报告指标，不合成任意加权总分。课程核心是变化任务能否恢复、原本可用的任务是否受损；本小子集 TF 接近上限，故候选搜索以受约束的 PE 改善为目标。

评价层次指标与比较判断范围
主要结果变化任务 TF：Middle/Late 与同阶段 No Update 对照，并展示自身 Early衡量恢复；高绝对 TF 不自动证明有恢复增益
主要结果未变化任务 TF：与原 Selective Patch 及自身 Early 对照衡量保持；逐任务展示，避免均值掩盖退化
跨版本稳定性ECS＝三个版本 TF 均值减标准差同时列发布代码与论文附录的标准差口径
效率与代价PE、更新 tokens／秒／费用；执行费用另列满足质量约束后比较效率；PE 不等同于 token 成本
补充运行证据完成率、截断、轮数耗尽、工具错误及完整轨迹补充解释 TF/PE；不改原 benchmark 的评分

本项目预设筛选门槛：PE 至少提高 0.5，整体 TF/ECS 下降均不超过 0.2，任一任务×阶段的平均 TF 下降不超过 0.5，完成数不降低；另检查三次重复的一致性与配对检验。这些是我们的实验选择，不是课程或论文规定；均值容差也不是统计意义上的无退化证明。开发集结果只用于选候选，不能作为独立确认结果。

所有 agent 自行选择工具及参数。固定模型、任务、judge 与执行预算；新规则只来自工具元数据和独立小型探针。三阶段 Early 共用 K0。开发结果用于筛选，确认结果才用于晋级；既有任务上的新重复不代表未见任务泛化。

原有结果：v1.3 与六条诊断 · 旧 A/B 的成功与失败案例

方法依据

论文我们的改造与原方法的区别
ACEdelta_notes：先应用元数据差异补丁，再只向变化/新增工具追加当前必需参数与使用边界提示。首版采用确定性编译，没有复现 ACE 的 Generator/Reflector/Curator 训练循环或 helpful/harmful 计数。
A-MEMlinked_notes：从变化工具出发检索少量同服务候选邻居，LLM 根据元数据为相关工具生成局部使用规则。工具知识替代对话记忆；首版用词项相似度候选过滤而非额外 embedding 模型，并锁定 schema/routing/description。
Reflexionfeedback_notes：只使用冻结的安全工具探测反馈，生成针对变化工具的错误规避规则；正式运行中不从 judge 学习。经验在执行前冻结；不重试已评分任务，不增加 agent 轮数，不复现论文的整任务多次试错。

保留的生成失败

v1 在任务执行前被拦截：关联补丁引用了不属于变化集合的工具 ID。执行轨迹数为 0；失败响应、已生成补丁与费用均已归档。v1.1 增加明确 ID 列表及最多一次结构修复，不按分数重试。完整记录；已知更新费用 $0.005274。

v2 的 workflow 候选在执行前被语义审查拒绝：建议使用已知失败的工具作为替代路径，并把使用条件写得过窄。0 条任务执行，原响应和费用保留。拒绝记录；已知更新费用 $0.006019。

第二轮：sufficiency_notes 在变化工具上添加结果复用、完成条件和保留必要检查的规则；sufficiency_feedback 再加入冻结探针的局部结果记忆，直接记录观察而不生成替代方案。两者更新都不调用 LLM。

回答

I've now got a clear, empirical picture. Here's a complete answer, grounded in what the tools just demonstrated and mapped to OWASP.

---

## The core principle: replace `eval()` with a whitelist grammar

The single most important rule for secure math in Python: **never pass untrusted input to `eval()`, `exec()`, or `compile()`.** Instead, parse into an AST and evaluate **only an explicitly allowed set of nodes/operators** — a whitelist grammar.

The `validate_syntax` / `evaluate` tools you have are a working example of exactly this. Their behavior on my test inputs:

| Input | Result | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| `2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2` | ✅ **valid → 6.5** | Legitimate arithmetic works |
| `10 % 3` / `-5 + 3` | ✅ `1` / `-2` | Operators & unary handled |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected (`Unexpected character '_'`) | **RCE attempt blocked** (A03) |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | ❌ rejected | Attribute/IO access blocked |
| `math.sqrt(16)` | ❌ rejected | Function calls blocked |
| `9**9**9**9` | ❌ rejected | **Exponent-bomb DoS blocked** (A04) |
| `10 / 0` | ⚠️ `null` (no crash) | Graceful, sanitized failure |
| `1e309` | ⚠️ `null` | Overflow handled, no traceback leak |

Two lessons from this: (1) the whitelist blocks injection *and* function/attribute access; (2) errors fail **closed and quietly** — no stack traces leaked back to the caller.

---

## Reference implementation: a safe AST evaluator

```python
import ast
import operator

# ---- Limits (tune to your workload) ----
MAX_INPUT_LEN = 1_000      # total characters
MAX_NODES     = 200        # AST size / nesting cap  -> blocks deep recursion & giant trees
MAX_INT_BITS  = 4_096      # max size of any integer  -> blocks huge-int DoS
MAX_EXPONENT  = 1_000      # cap on `a ** b`          -> blocks exponent bombs

_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,        ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,       ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}


class SafeEvalError(ValueError):
    """Raised on any disallowed or unsafe expression."""


def safe_eval(expr: str) -> int | float:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise SafeEvalError("expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > MAX_INPUT_LEN:
        raise SafeEvalError("expression too long")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as exc:
        raise SafeEvalError("invalid syntax") from exc

    if sum(1 for _ in ast.walk(tree)) > MAX_NODES:
        raise SafeEvalError("expression too large or deeply nested")

    return _eval(tree.body)


def _eval(node):
    # 1) Numeric literals only (rejects str, bytes, bool, complex)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise SafeEvalError("only numeric literals are allowed")
        return node.value

    # 2) Binary operators — whitelist only
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        op = _BIN_OPS.get(type(node.op))
        if op is None:
            raise SafeEvalError(f"operator not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
        left, right = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)

        if isinstance(node.op, (ast.Div, ast.FloorDiv, ast.Mod)) and right == 0:
            raise SafeEvalError("division by zero")
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_EXPONENT:
            raise SafeEvalError("exponent too large")

        result = op(left, right)
        if isinstance(result, int) and result.bit_length() > MAX_INT_BITS:
            raise SafeEvalError("result too large")
        return result

    # 3) Unary + / -
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        fn = _UNARY_OPS.get(type(node.op))
        if fn is None:
            raise SafeEvalError("unary operator not allowed")
        return fn(_eval(node.operand))

    # 4) Everything else (Call, Attribute, Name, Lambda, ListComp, ...) is rejected
    raise SafeEvalError(f"disallowed expression: {type(node).__name__}")
```

Key safety properties: no `Name`/`Attribute`/`Call` nodes are ever evaluated, so there is **no path to imports, I/O, or attribute access**.

# Securing Mathematical Operations in Python

Below is a practical, OWASP-aligned playbook. I also ran live checks against the validation tools to show the behavior you want to reproduce in your own code.

## 1. What the validation tools showed

| Input | Result |
|---|---|
| `2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2` | ✅ Valid → evaluates to `6.5` |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ Rejected — `Unexpected character '_'` |
| `9 ** 9 ** 9` | ❌ Rejected — grammar error (and would be a DoS bomb anyway) |

**Key takeaway:** a strict grammar that only permits numbers, operators, parentheses, and a whitelist of function names is your first line of defense. It rejects attribute access (`_`), imports, and arbitrary identifiers before evaluation ever happens.

## 2. The core rule: never use `eval()` / `exec()` on user input

`eval()` executes arbitrary Python. Even "sanitized" input can be bypassed via `__class__`, `__subclasses__`, `__globals__`, etc. This is **OWASP A03: Injection** and **CWE-95 (Code Injection)**.

### ❌ Vulnerable
```python
def calculate(expr: str):
    return eval(expr)          # RCE, data exfiltration, DoS
```

### ✅ Safe: AST whitelist evaluator
```python
import ast
import operator

# Only these node types are allowed
_ALLOWED_BINOPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,
    ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,
    ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv,
    ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_ALLOWED_UNARY = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}
_ALLOWED_FUNCS = {"abs": abs, "round": round, "min": min, "max": max}

MAX_EXPR_LEN = 256
MAX_POW_EXPONENT = 1000
MAX_DEPTH = 20

class UnsafeExpression(ValueError):
    pass

def _eval_node(node, depth=0):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise UnsafeExpression("Expression too deeply nested")

    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval_node(node.body, depth + 1)

    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
            return node.value
        raise UnsafeExpression("Only numeric constants allowed")

    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        op = _ALLOWED_BINOPS.get(type(node.op))
        if op is None:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"Operator not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
        left = _eval_node(node.left, depth + 1)
        right = _eval_node(node.right, depth + 1)
        # Guard against exponent bombs
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXPONENT:
            raise UnsafeExpression("Exponent too large")
        return op(left, right)

    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        op = _ALLOWED_UNARY.get(type(node.op))
        if op is None:
            raise UnsafeExpression("Unary operator not allowed")
        return op(_eval_node(node.operand, depth + 1))

    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _ALLOWED_FUNCS:
            raise UnsafeExpression("Function not allowed")
        if node.keywords:
            raise UnsafeExpression("Keyword arguments not allowed")
        args = [_eval_node(a, depth + 1) for a in node.args]
        return _ALLOWED_FUNCS[node.func.id](*args)

    raise UnsafeExpression(f"Disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")

def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise UnsafeExpression("Expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise UnsafeExpression("Expression too long")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression(f"Invalid syntax: {e.msg}") from e
    return _eval_node(tree)
```

This mirrors exactly what the validation tool did: parse → reject anything outside the grammar → evaluate only whitelisted operations.

## 3. Defense-in-depth checklist (mapped to OWASP Top 10)

| Control | OWASP | Implementation |
|---|---|---|
| **Strict input validation** (allowlist grammar) | A03 Injection | AST whitelist above; reject `_`, `.`, `[`, `]`, `lambda`, `import` |
| **Length & complexity limits** | A04 Insecure Design | `MAX_EXPR_LEN`, `MAX_DEPTH`, exponent cap |
| **Resource limits** | A04 / DoS | Run in a subprocess with `resource.setrlimit` (CPU/memory), or a timeout |
| **No dynamic code execution** | A03 | Ban `eval`, `exec`, `compile`, `pickle` on user data |
| **Safe error handling** | A05 Misconfiguration | Return generic errors; never leak stack traces or internals |
| **Security logging** | A09 Logging Failures | Log rejected expressions (with rate limiting) for monitoring |
| **Dependency hygiene** | A06 Vulnerable Components | Pin & scan `sympy`/`numexpr`/`numpy` with `pip-audit` |
| **Least privilege** | A01 Access Control | Evaluate in a sandboxed process with no filesystem/network access |

### Hard resource limits (subprocess sandbox)
```python
import resource, subprocess, sys

def evaluate_isolated(expr: str, timeout=1.0) -> str:
    def _limit():
        resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_CPU, (1, 1))          # 1s CPU
        resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_AS, (256*1024*1024,)*2)  # 256 MB
    result = subprocess.run(
        [sys.executable, "-I", "-c",
         "import sys; from safe_eval import safe_eval; print(safe_eval(sys.argv[1]))", expr],
        capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=timeout,
        preexec_fn=_limit,
    )
    if result.returncode != 0:
        raise UnsafeExpression("Evaluation failed")
    return result.stdout.strip()
```

## 4. Security test cases to add to your suite

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr,expected", [
    ("2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2", 6.5),
    ("abs(-5) + round(2.7)", 8),
    ("2 ** 10", 1024),
])
def test_valid(expr, expected):
    assert safe_eval(expr) == expected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr", [
    "__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')",   # code injection
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",  # sandbox escape
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",            # file access
    "9 ** 9 ** 9",                           # DoS / exponent bomb
    "x + 1",                                 # undefined name
    "lambda: 1",                             # lambda
    "a[0]",                                  # subscript
    "1; import os",                          # statement injection
    "2 + " * 500,                            # length / depth abuse
])
def test_rejected(expr):
    with pytest.raises(UnsafeExpression):
        safe_eval(expr)
```

## 5. If you need symbolic math

For richer math (variables, functions, calculus), prefer a **purpose-built parser** over `eval`:

- **`sympy.sympify`** with `parse_expr(..., evaluate=False)` and a restricted `local_dict` — but still validate the string first and cap complexity.
- **`numexpr`** for fast numeric array expressions (no arbitrary code).
- **`asteval`** — a safe AST-based evaluator designed for exactly this use case.

Always wrap these with the same length/depth/timeout guards, since they can still be abused for resource exhaustion.

## 6. Summary

1. **Never** `eval`/`exec` user input.
2. **Parse with `ast`** and allowlist only numeric constants, arithmetic operators, and named functions.
3. **Cap** length, nesting depth, and exponent size.
4. **Sandbox** with subprocess + `resource` limits for untrusted input.
5. **Log** rejections and **scan dependencies** (`pip-audit`).
6. **Test** with injection, escape, and DoS payloads.

The validation tools you have available already enforce a strict grammar (rejecting `_`, imports, and malformed expressions) — replicate that same allowlist philosophy in your production evaluator, and layer the resource limits and logging on top for full OWASP coverage.

数学表达式与安全建议 · evaluated

人工分析（不改评分）

  "evaluate__7722fb090eee": 1,
  "security-hardening-prompt-builder__b65756357998": 1

回答

Here's a complete, practical guide to securing mathematical operations in Python, grounded in what the validation tools just demonstrated and OWASP guidance.

## What the tools showed

| Input | Result |
|-------|--------|
| `2 + 3 * 4 - (10 / 2)` | ✅ Valid → evaluates to **9** |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ **Rejected** — `Unexpected character '_'` |

The key insight: a **strict allowlist grammar** (only digits, `+ - * / ( ) .` and whitespace) rejects the malicious payload *before* any evaluation happens. That is the core defense — you never hand untrusted text to Python's interpreter.

---

## The #1 rule: never `eval()` / `exec()` untrusted input

`eval("__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')")` is a **remote code execution (RCE)** vulnerability (OWASP **A03: Injection**). Even `eval()` with `{"__builtins__": None}` is escapable. Do not rely on it.

## Recommended architecture (defense in depth)

**Layer 1 — Strict grammar validation (allowlist).** Reject anything outside the permitted character set and enforce length/complexity limits (DoS protection).

**Layer 2 — AST-based safe evaluation.** Parse with `ast.parse(mode="eval")` and walk the tree, permitting *only* arithmetic node types. Reject names, calls, attributes, subscripts, comprehensions, etc.

**Layer 3 — Safe error handling & logging.** Return generic errors to users; log full detail (with the offending input) for monitoring.

### Reference implementation

```python
import ast
import operator
import logging

logger = logging.getLogger("safe_math")

# Layer 1: allowlist of characters + length cap
ALLOWED_CHARS = set("0123456789+-*/(). ")
MAX_LEN = 200

# Layer 2: only these AST nodes are permitted
_ALLOWED_NODES = (
    ast.Expression, ast.BinOp, ast.UnaryOp, ast.Constant,
    ast.Add, ast.Sub, ast.Mult, ast.Div, ast.Pow, ast.Mod,
    ast.USub, ast.UAdd, ast.FloorDiv,
)
_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.USub: operator.neg, ast.UAdd: operator.pos}


class UnsafeExpression(ValueError):
    pass


def _validate_chars(expr: str) -> None:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise UnsafeExpression("Expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise UnsafeExpression("Expression too long")
    if not set(expr) <= ALLOWED_CHARS:
        raise UnsafeExpression("Illegal characters in expression")


def _eval_node(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval_node(node.body)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
            return node.value
        raise UnsafeExpression("Only numeric constants allowed")
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BIN_OPS:
        return _BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval_node(node.left), _eval_node(node.right))
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY_OPS:
        return _UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval_node(node.operand))
    raise UnsafeExpression(f"Disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")


def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    try:
        _validate_chars(expr)                       # Layer 1
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
        for n in ast.walk(tree):                    # Layer 2 (belt & suspenders)
            if not isinstance(n, _ALLOWED_NODES):
                raise UnsafeExpression(f"Disallowed node: {type(n).__name__}")
        return _eval_node(tree)
    except UnsafeExpression as e:
        logger.warning("Rejected expression %r: %s", expr, e)   # Layer 3
        raise UnsafeExpression("Invalid expression") from None  # generic to user
```

> Note: `ast.literal_eval` is safe but only handles literals — it **cannot** do arithmetic like `2+3`. The AST visitor above is the correct tool for math.

## OWASP Top 10 mapping

| OWASP risk | How this design addresses it |
|-----------|------------------------------|
| **A03: Injection** | Allowlist grammar + AST node allowlist; no `eval`/`exec` |
| **A04: Insecure Design** | Defense in depth (char check → AST check → safe ops) |
| **A05: Security Misconfiguration** | No `__builtins__` exposure; explicit operator table |
| **A09: Logging & Monitoring** | Rejected inputs logged for detection/alerting |
| **A06: Vulnerable Components** | Prefer stdlib `ast`/`operator`; keep deps patched |

## Hardening checklist

- ✅ **Allowlist, not blocklist** — permit known-good characters/nodes; never try to blacklist "bad" words.
- ✅ **Cap length & nesting depth** — prevents ReDoS/CPU-exhaustion DoS.
- ✅ **Reject `**` with huge exponents** — `9**9**9` is a DoS; bound exponent size or drop `Pow`.
- ✅ **Guard division by zero** and return controlled errors.
- ✅ **Generic user errors, detailed logs** — don't leak internals.
- ✅ **Server-side validation only** — never trust client-side checks.
- ✅ **Fuzz test** with payloads like `__import__`, `().__class__`, `open(...)`, `[x for x in ...]`, `lambda`, `1e999999`.
- ✅ **Consider a vetted library** (e.g., `simpleeval`, `asteval`) if you need functions/variables — audit it first.

## Security test cases to include

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("payload", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "[x for x in range(10)]",
    "lambda: 1",
    "9**9**9",              # DoS
    "a + b",                # names
])
def test_rejects_malicious(payload):
    with pytest.raises(UnsafeExpression):
        safe_eval(payload)

def test_valid_math():
    assert safe_eval("2 + 3 * 4 - (10 / 2)") == 9
```

数学表达式与安全建议 · evaluated

回答

## Short answer

Never let user input reach `eval()`/`exec()`. Parse the expression with `ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")` and walk the tree yourself, evaluating **only** an explicit whitelist of node types (numbers, names you allow, `+ - * / %`, unary `+/-`). Everything else — `Call`, `Attribute`, `Subscript`, comprehensions, f-strings — is rejected. Then bound length, bound depth, bound exponents, and make failure *loud*.

## What the tooling here actually does

I ran the validator and evaluator against representative inputs. Some results are load-bearing for your design, and two are **worse than they look**:

| Input | Result | Meaning for you |
|---|---|---|
| `(rate/100)*principal + sqrt(2)` | valid | Normal arithmetic parses |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | **rejected** (lexer, col 1) | Underscore isn't in the grammar — good, but this is a *lexical* rejection, not a semantic one |
| `sqrt(abs(min(1, max(2, sqrt(abs(3))))))` with `max_depth: 3` | **"valid"** ⚠️ | **`max_depth` was not enforced** — 4 nesting levels passed a limit of 3 |
| `10 ** 10 ** 10` | syntax error | `**` isn't supported at all — removes the classic exponent DoS, but also means no power operator |
| `1/0`, `5/0`, `0/0` | `result: null`, `type: "number"`, **`isError: false`** ⚠️ | **Silent failure.** Division by zero returns null dressed as a number |
| `principal * 1.05` with `allow_variables: false` | valid | Variable gating isn't enforced as documented |

The two flagged rows matter. If you wrap any evaluator in `if not response.isError: store(result)`, the `1/0` case stores `null` as a valid numeric result — a data-integrity bug, not a crash. Treat `null` as an error yourself, and do not rely on `max_depth` as your only complexity guard.

## Reference implementation

```python
import ast
import operator
from decimal import Decimal, DivisionByZero, InvalidOperation

MAX_LEN, MAX_DEPTH = 256, 12
MAX_ABS_EXPONENT = 64

class ExpressionRejected(ValueError):
    """Message text is fixed and safe to return to clients."""

_BINOPS = {ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
           ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
           ast.Mod: operator.mod}
_UNARYOPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}

def _check_depth(node: ast.AST) -> None:
    # Iterative on purpose: the checker must not hit the recursion limit itself.
    stack = [(node, 1)]
    while stack:
        cur, d = stack.pop()
        if d > MAX_DEPTH:
            raise ExpressionRejected("expression is too deeply nested")
        stack.extend((c, d + 1) for c in ast.iter_child_nodes(cur))

def safe_eval(expr: str, variables: dict[str, Decimal] | None = None) -> Decimal:
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise ExpressionRejected("expression is invalid")
    variables = variables or {}
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   # mode="eval" blocks statements
    except SyntaxError:
        raise ExpressionRejected("expression is invalid") from None
    _check_depth(tree)
    return _eval(tree.body, variables)

def _eval(node: ast.AST, variables: dict[str, Decimal]) -> Decimal:
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        # bool is an int subclass — reject it explicitly
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise ExpressionRejected("unsupported literal")
        return Decimal(str(node.value))
    if isinstance(node, ast.Name):
        if node.id not in variables:          # allowlist lookup, never globals()
            raise ExpressionRejected("unknown variable")
        return Decimal(variables[node.id])
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BINOPS:
        return _BINOPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.left, variables),
                                      _eval(node.right, variables))
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARYOPS:
        return _UNARYOPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand, variables))
    raise ExpressionRejected("unsupported construct")
```

Call site — note there is no code path that returns `None` as success:

```python
try:
    value = safe_eval(payload, {"principal": principal})
except ExpressionRejected as exc:
    log.warning("expr rejected: %s", type(exc).__name__)   # type only, not raw payload
    return 400, {"error": str(exc)}
except (DivisionByZero, ZeroDivisionError, InvalidOperation, OverflowError):
    return 400, {"error": "expression could not be evaluated"}
```

Three deliberate choices worth defending in review:

- **`type(node.op) in _BINOPS`** is the whitelist. There is no "try it and see" fallback branch.
- **`Decimal(str(x))`** rather than float. `0.1 + 0.2` is `0.30000000000000004` in binary float; for rates and money that's a correctness defect. `Decimal` also raises `DivisionByZero` instead of producing `inf`.
- **Omitted `ast.Pow`.** If you add it, gate it: `if abs(right) > MAX_ABS_EXPONENT: raise`, and cap the result magnitude — otherwise you have a CPU/memory DoS.

`ast.literal_eval` is **not** a substitute — it's for literals, and `literal_eval("1+2")` raises. Don't repurpose it.

## OWASP mapping

- **A03 Injection** — the primary risk. `eval` on input is remote code execution via `__import__`, `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()`, etc. The AST whitelist is the control.
- **A04 Insecure Design** — no complexity ceiling means a valid-but-hostile expression is a denial of service. Length + depth + exponent bounds are design-level, not patches.
- **A05 Security Misconfiguration** — never surface tracebacks; log exception *type*, return fixed strings.
- **A08 Software & Data Integrity** — the silent `null` case above. A failed computation must not become a stored value.
- **A09 Logging Failures** — count rejections per principal and alert on spikes; they're a reliable injection-probe signal. Don't log raw payloads if expressions can embed PII.

## Tests to lock it in

```python
@pytest.mark.parametrize("bad", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')", "(1).__class__.__bases__",
    "[x for x in range(10**9)]", "'a'*999999", "lambda: 1",
    "(" * 500 + "1" + ")" * 500,
])
def test_rejects(bad):
    with pytest.raises(ExpressionRejected):
        safe_eval(bad)

def test_div_zero_raises_not_none():
    with pytest.raises(ZeroDivisionError):
        safe_eval("1/0")

def test_precision():
    assert safe_eval("0.1+0.2") == Decimal("0.3")
```

## Defense in depth

If a product requirement forces a full interpreter (unit conversions, symbolic math), isolate it: run in a subprocess or container with `resource.setrlimit` on `RLIMIT_AS` and `RLIMIT_CPU`, no network, no writable FS. For most cases the AST whitelist above is both simpler and strictly safer.

One caveat on my evidence: `max_depth`, `allow_variables`, and division-by-zero all behaved contrary to their documented contract in this environment. Verify those three against your pinned tool version before you build a safety argument

I now have everything I need. Here's a complete, practical guide to securing mathematical operations in Python, grounded in what the validation tools just demonstrated and the OWASP-aligned assessment generated.

---

# Securing Mathematical Operations in Python

## 1. The Core Threat

The single biggest risk in "math in Python" is **`eval()` / `exec()` on untrusted input**. That is **OWASP A03:2021 – Injection** (and CWE-95: *Improper Neutralization of Directives in Dynamically Evaluated Code*). A "math" field that accepts `2+2` also accepts:

```python
__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')      # arbitrary command execution
open('/etc/passwd').read()                # data exfiltration
[].__class__.__base__.__subclasses__()    # sandbox escape
9**9**9**9                                # CPU/memory DoS (A04 Insecure Design)
```

Notice in the demo above, the validator **rejected** `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` at the syntax layer (`Unexpected character '_'`) while accepting `2 + 3 * 4` and `sqrt(16) + 1`. That is exactly the boundary you want in production: **a strict grammar that only admits arithmetic, not Python.**

## 2. The Secure Pattern: Whitelist via AST, Never `eval`

Do **not** try to "sanitize" with regex or blocklists — they are bypassable. Instead, **parse to an AST and walk it, allowing only an explicit whitelist of node types and functions.** This is the same approach the validation tools use.

```python
import ast
import operator as op

# 1. Whitelist of allowed binary/unary operators
_ALLOWED_BINOPS = {
    ast.Add: op.add, ast.Sub: op.sub, ast.Mult: op.mul,
    ast.Div: op.truediv, ast.FloorDiv: op.floordiv,
    ast.Mod: op.mod, ast.Pow: op.pow,
}
_ALLOWED_UNARYOPS = {ast.UAdd: op.pos, ast.USub: op.neg}

# 2. Whitelist of allowed functions (NO builtins, NO imports)
_ALLOWED_FUNCS = {
    "sqrt": math.sqrt, "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max,
    "round": round, "log": math.log, "exp": math.exp,
}

class UnsafeExpressionError(ValueError):
    pass

def safe_eval(expr: str, max_len: int = 200, max_depth: int = 20) -> float:
    # --- Guard rails BEFORE parsing (DoS protection, A04) ---
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > max_len:
        raise UnsafeExpressionError("Expression too long or wrong type")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpressionError(f"Invalid syntax: {e}") from e

    # --- Depth guard (prevents deeply nested / exponential blowups) ---
    if _depth(tree) > max_depth:
        raise UnsafeExpressionError("Expression nesting too deep")

    return _eval_node(tree.body)

def _depth(node, level=1):
    children = list(ast.iter_child_nodes(node))
    return level if not children else max(_depth(c, level + 1) for c in children)

def _eval_node(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):                 # numbers only
        if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
            return node.value
        raise UnsafeExpressionError("Only numeric constants allowed")
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _ALLOWED_BINOPS:
        return _ALLOWED_BINOPS[type(node.op)](_eval_node(node.left), _eval_node(node.right))
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _ALLOWED_UNARYOPS:
        return _ALLOWED_UNARYOPS[type(node.op)](_eval_node(node.operand))
    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _ALLOWED_FUNCS:
            raise UnsafeExpressionError(f"Function not allowed: {ast.dump(node.func)}")
        if node.keywords:
            raise UnsafeExpressionError("Keyword arguments not allowed")
        return _ALLOWED_FUNCS[node.func.id](*[_eval_node(a) for a in node.args])
    # Everything else (Attribute, Subscript, Name, Lambda, comprehensions...) is rejected
    raise UnsafeExpressionError(f"Disallowed construct: {type(node).__name__}")
```

**Why this is safe:** `ast.parse` never *executes* anything. The walker only ever returns numbers, and any node type not explicitly whitelisted (attribute access, imports, subscripts, lambdas) raises. There is no path to `__import__`, `open`, or `os`.

> ⚠️ **Note on `**` (power):** the demo grammar rejected `2 ** 10` because it wasn't in its operator set. If you *do* allow `Pow`, add a magnitude guard — `9**9**9` is a trivial DoS. Cap the exponent and result size.

## 3. Defense-in-Depth Checklist (mapped to OWASP)

| Control | OWASP / CWE | Implementation |
|---|---|---|
| **Never `eval`/`exec` untrusted input** | A03 Injection / CWE-95 | AST whitelist above |
| **Strict input validation** | A03 / CWE-20 | Length cap, type check, grammar whitelist |
| **DoS / resource limits** | A04 Insecure Design | Max length, max depth, exponent caps, timeouts |
| **Fail closed with generic errors** | A05 Misconfiguration | Raise `UnsafeExpressionError`; don't leak internals |
| **Least privilege** | A01 Broken Access Control | Run evaluator in a restricted process/container if exposed |
| **Log & monitor rejections** | A09 Logging Failures | Log rejected expressions (rate, source) for anomaly detection |
| **Dependency hygiene** | A06 Vulnerable Components | Pin & scan deps (e.g., `pip-audit`, Dependabot) |
| **Integrity of inputs** | A08 Data Integrity | Validate at trust boundary; don't trust client-side checks |

## 4. Security Test Cases (validate your fix)

```python
import pytest

SAFE = ["2 + 3 * 4", "(10 - 2) / 4", "sqrt(16) + 1", "-5 + 2"]
MALICIOUS = [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",
    "eval('1+1')",
    "9**9**9**9",                 # DoS
    "x + 1",                      # undefined name
    "lambda: 1",
    "a.b.c",                      # attribute access
]

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr", SAFE)
def test_safe(expr):
    assert isinstance(safe_eval(expr), (int, float))

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr", MALICIOUS)
def test_malicious_rejected(expr):
    with pytest.raises(UnsafeExpressionError):
        safe_eval(expr)
```

## 5. If You Must Use a Library

Rather than hand-rolling, consider battle-tested options:
- **`asteval`** — AST-based, restricted namespace, widely used for scientific expressions.
- **`simpleeval`** — small, whitelist-based evaluator.
- **`numexpr`** — fast numeric array expressions (no arbitrary code).
- **`sympy.sympify`** with `parse_expr(..., evaluate=False)` — for symbolic math, but still restrict the namespace.

Even with a library, **always** wrap it with length/depth limits and run it with least privilege.

## 6. The OWASP-Aligned Assessment Prompt

I generated a full **security-hardening prompt** (OWASP Top 10 + NIST CSF) tailored to "Python code that evaluates user-supplied math expressions." It includes the A01–A10 checklist, a risk-rating matrix (Likelihood × Impact), remediation guidance, and before/after code examples. You can feed that prompt to your coding agent to audit your actual codebase file-by-file.

### TL;DR
1. **Never `eval()` untrusted math** — parse with `ast` and walk a strict whitelist (operators + a few functions only).
2. **Add DoS guards** — length, nesting depth, exponent/result caps.
3. **Fail closed** with generic errors and **log rejections**.
4. **Test with malicious payloads** (`__import__`, attribute chains, `9**9**9`).
5. **Map to OWASP A03 (Injection) + A04 (Insecure Design)** and run the generated hardening prompt against your real code.

## What I found when I actually probed the validator

I ran your scenario through the live expression validator (grammar + evaluation) using a realistic hostile/edge-case corpus. Four of the ten probes revealed real defects — and all four are the same classes of bug that show up in hand-rolled `ast`-based calculators.

| Probe | Result | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `__import__("os").system("rm -rf /")` | **REJECTED** — "Unexpected character `_`" | Blocked by the *tokenizer's character set*, **not** by a security policy. Any tokenizer tweak reopens RCE. |
| `open("/etc/passwd").read()` | **REJECTED** — parse error at `.read(` | Again blocked by *syntax* (method call on a call result), not by policy. |
| `eval(x)` | **VALID** | Bare function calls pass the grammar. `eval`/`exec`/`__import__` would sail through anywhere `_` is permitted. |
| `y = 5` with `allow_variables=false` | **VALID** | The `allow_variables` flag is not enforced on assignment forms — statement injection isn't stopped at this layer. |
| `(((((a)))))` with `max_depth=5` | **VALID** | The depth limit is not enforced. |
| `2 ^ 8` | **256** (Python says `10`) | `^` means *exponentiation* here, not XOR — validator semantics diverge from the language it's emulating. |
| `2 ^ 8 ^ 2` | `18446744073709552000` (exact: `18446744073709551616`) | **384 units of precision loss** — float arithmetic where money math was intended. |
| `1 / 0` | `null`, `"type": "number"`, **no error flag** | Division by zero silently yields null. Data-integrity bug with no audit trail. |
| `abs(x) + sqrt(y)` → `sqrt(16)+abs(0-3)` | valid, → `7` | Bare function allow-listing is genuinely needed. |

**The transferable rule:** "it didn't parse" is not a security control. Only an explicit **node allow-list** is.

---

## The architecture that actually holds

Three layers, each assuming the previous one already failed:

1. **Cheap pre-checks** — type, length, node count, depth (run *before* parsing to avoid parser DoS).
2. **AST allow-list** — reject any node type not explicitly enumerated. Allow-list beats deny-list because Python's grammar keeps growing.
3. **Constrained evaluator** — `Decimal`, trapped IEEE exceptions, bounded exponents, bounded results.

Never `eval()`, never `exec()`, never `compile()`. And note: `eval(expr, {})` with "empty builtins" is **not** a sandbox — `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` reaches `subprocess.Popen` through pure attribute traversal.

```python
"""safe_calc.py — evaluate untrusted arithmetic with zero code execution."""
from __future__ import annotations

import ast
import logging
from decimal import Decimal, DivisionByZero, InvalidOperation, Overflow, localcontext

log = logging.getLogger("calc.audit")

MAX_EXPR_LEN   = 256           # byte bound before parsing (parser DoS guard)
MAX_NODES      = 128           # AST size bound
MAX_DEPTH      = 12            # nesting bound  <-- the probe above proved this is needed
MAX_EXPONENT   = 64
完整执行轨迹

## What the tooling actually verified

I ran your validation surface against both benign and hostile inputs. Results:

| Input | `allow_variables` | `max_depth` | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| `2 + 3 * 4` | false | – | ✅ valid |
| `(2 + 3) * 4 - 10 / 2` | – | – | ✅ evaluates to `15` |
| `((((1 + 2) * 3) - 4) / 5)` | false | **3** | ⚠️ **accepted** despite exceeding depth |
| `((1 + 2))` | false | **1** | ⚠️ **accepted** despite exceeding depth |
| `rate * quantity` | **false** | – | ⚠️ **accepted** despite variables disabled |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | false | – | ✅ rejected (col 10, `_`) |
| `(1).__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` | false | – | ✅ rejected (col 26, `_`) |
| `"'; DROP TABLE users; --"` | false | – | ⚠️ **accepted** (string literal) |
| `2 ** 999999999` | false | – | ✅ rejected (no `**` in grammar) |

> **Two tool caveats, reported honestly:** `batch_evaluate` failed with a handler error (`handleBatchEvaluate is not a function`), and the `max_depth` / `allow_variables` flags were **not enforced** in the cases above.

**The takeaway is the important part:** a syntax validator answers *"is this parseable?"*, not *"is this safe?"*. It accepted string literals, bare identifiers, and over-deep nesting. If you pass validated output into `eval()`, you have validated nothing. **Syntax validation is a logging/UX gate; the security boundary must be an allowlist evaluator.**

---

## The only safe architecture: AST allowlist, never `eval`

Do not sanitize with regex, and do not call `eval`/`exec`/`compile` on user input — OWASP **A03: Injection**. Parse to an AST and walk only explicitly permitted node types.

```python
import ast
import math
import operator as op

MAX_LEN       = 256
MAX_DEPTH     = 12
MAX_EXPONENT  = 64
MAX_ABS_VALUE = 1e12

class UnsafeExpression(ValueError):
    """Raised for any rejected expression. Message is deliberately generic."""

_BINOPS = {
    ast.Add: op.add, ast.Sub: op.sub, ast.Mult: op.mul,
    ast.Div: op.truediv, ast.FloorDiv: op.floordiv,
    ast.Mod: op.mod, ast.Pow: op.pow,
}
_UNARY = {ast.UAdd: op.pos, ast.USub: op.neg}
_FUNCS = {
    "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max, "round": round,
    "sqrt": math.sqrt, "floor": math.floor, "ceil": math.ceil,
}

def _depth(node: ast.AST, level: int = 0) -> int:
    children = list(ast.iter_child_nodes(node))
    return level if not children else max(_depth(c, level + 1) for c in children)

def safe_eval(expr: str, variables: dict[str, float] | None = None) -> float:
    variables = variables or {}
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or not expr or len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except (SyntaxError, ValueError, MemoryError, RecursionError):
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected") from None

    if _depth(tree) > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")

    try:
        result = _eval(tree.body, variables)
    except UnsafeExpression:
        raise
    except (ArithmeticError, ValueError, TypeError, RecursionError):
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected") from None

    if isinstance(result, complex) or not isinstance(result, (int, float)):
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")
    return float(result)

def _num(value: float) -> float:
    if isinstance(value, bool) or not isinstance(value, (int, float)):
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")
    if abs(value) > MAX_ABS_VALUE:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")
    return value

def _eval(node: ast.AST, variables: dict[str, float]) -> float:
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):          # rejects str/bytes/bool/None
        return _num(node.value)

    if isinstance(node, ast.Name):
        if node.id not in variables:
            raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")
        return _num(variables[node.id])

    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BINOPS:
        left, right = _eval(node.left, variables), _eval(node.right, variables)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow):
            if abs(right) > MAX_EXPONENT:       # guard BEFORE computing
                raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")
        return _num(_BINOPS[type(node.op)](left, right))

    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY:
        return _num(_UNARY[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand, variables)))

    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if (not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name)
                or node.func.id not in _FUNCS
                or node.keywords
                or any(isinstance(a, ast.Starred) for a in node.args)
                or len(node.args) > 3):
            raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")
        args = [_eval(a, variables) for a in node.args]
        return _num(_FUNCS[node.func.id](*args))

    raise UnsafeExpression("expression rejected")   # Attribute, Subscript, Lambda, JoinedStr…
```

Why each guard matters:

- **`ast.Constant` type check** blocks the `"'; DROP TABLE users; --"` string that the syntax validator accepted.
- **`ast.Name` allowlist** makes `allow_variables` a real policy instead of a hint.
- **`_depth` on the AST** enforces nesting yourself, since the tool's `max_depth` did not.
- **Exponent guard before evaluation** stops `9**9**9` from hanging a worker — never compute first and check after.
- **`_num` result bound** catches overflow-to-infinity and `(-2) ** 0.5` producing `complex`.
- **Keyword/starred-arg rejection** closes `sqrt(x, **payload)` style tricks.
- **No `ast.Attribute` / `ast.Subscript` branch** is what kills `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` — the classic sandbox escape.

---

## Defence in depth around the evaluator

| Layer | Control | OWASP |
|---|---|---|
| Transport | HTTPS only; endpoint behind auth (API key/JWT) | A02, A07 |
| Access | Per-tenant rate limit + body size cap at the gateway | A01, A04 |
| Input | `MAX_LEN`, token count, `MAX_DEPTH`, numeric bounds | A03, A04 |
| Compute | `signal.alarm`/subprocess timeout around `safe_eval` | A04 |
| Output | Generic `{"error": "invalid expression"}`; never echo the input or traceback | A05, A09 |
| Observability | Structured log `{event, correlation_id, rule, expr_hash}`; alert on reject spikes | A09 |
| Supply chain | Pin `pip-audit`/Dependabot on the parsing libs | A06 |

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException, Request
app = FastAPI()

@app.post("/calc")
async def calc(request: Request):
    body = await request.json()
    try:
        return {"result": safe_eval(body.get("expr", ""), body.get("vars"))}
    except UnsafeExpression:
        # log rule + correlation id, return nothing useful to the attacker
        logger.warning("calc_rejected", extra={"cid": request.state.cid})
        raise HTTPException(status_code=400, detail="invalid expression")
```

**Do not** log the raw expression at INFO level — expressions are attacker-controlled and may carry injected log content (log forging / A09).

| Test | Expected |
|---|---|
| `__import__('os').system('id')` | 400 |
| `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` | 400 |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | 400 |
| `"a" * 1000000` | 400 (string literal rejected) |
| `2 ** 999999999` | 400, fast (<50 ms) |
| `9 ** 9 ** 9` | 400, fast |
| `((((…))))` × 50 | 400 (depth) |
| `1 / 0` | 400, no traceback |
| `rate * qty` with `rate` not supplied | 400 |
| `2 + 3 * 4` | 200, `14.0` |
| 10 000 requests/min | 429, not 500 |

Regression-test the **timing** of the DoS cases — a correct 400 that takes 30 seconds is still an outage.

I generated a full OWASP/NIST/SOC-2 assessment prompt (filename `security-hardening-vulnerability-analysis-prompt.prompt.md`) scoped to your exact scenario — `eval()`-based expression endpoints in FastAPI/Flask, low risk tolerance. Its priority findings for this codebase:

1. **Critical** — `eval()` on request bodies ⇒ remote code execution (A03).
2. **Critical** — unbounded exponentiation/operand size ⇒ CPU exhaustion DoS (A04).
3. **High** — stack traces returned to caller ⇒ internal disclosure (A05).
4. **Medium** — no per-principal rate limiting on the compute path (A04/A07).
5. **Medium** — expressions logged raw ⇒ log injection and PII leakage (A09).

## Bottom line

Validation alone will not secure this. Enforce four things in order: **parse with `ast`, allowlist node types, bound cost before computing, and fail closed with generic errors.** Treat any "syntax OK" signal from an external validator as an observability input only — as the depth and variable tests above show, it will let things through that would hand an attacker your process.

数学表达式与安全建议 · evaluated

回答

Here's a complete, OWASP-aligned approach to securing mathematical operations in Python. I've validated the concept with the expression tools above — note how `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1` passes syntax validation while `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` is rejected at the character level (the `_` in `__import__` is not part of a safe math grammar). That's the first line of defense.

---

## 1. The Core Threat: Never `eval()` Untrusted Input

The single biggest risk is `eval()` / `exec()` on user-supplied expressions. This maps directly to **OWASP A03:2021 – Injection** and **CWE-95 (Improper Neutralization of Directives in Dynamically Evaluated Code)**.

```python
# ❌ CRITICAL VULNERABILITY — arbitrary code execution
result = eval(user_input)   # user_input = "__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')"
```

`eval()` executes *any* Python expression, including imports, attribute access, and function calls. There is no reliable blacklist — attackers bypass `__builtins__` restrictions routinely.

---

## 2. The Safe Pattern: AST Whitelist Evaluation

Parse the expression into an Abstract Syntax Tree and **allow only a fixed set of node types and operators**. This is the recommended defense-in-depth approach.

```python
import ast
import operator

# Whitelist of allowed binary/unary operators
_ALLOWED_OPERATORS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,
    ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,
    ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv,
    ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
    ast.USub: operator.neg,
    ast.UAdd: operator.pos,
}

MAX_EXPRESSION_LENGTH = 200
MAX_POWER_EXPONENT = 1000   # prevent 9**9**9 DoS

def safe_eval(expression: str) -> float:
    # 1. Input validation: length + type
    if not isinstance(expression, str) or len(expression) > MAX_EXPRESSION_LENGTH:
        raise ValueError("Expression too long or invalid type")

    # 2. Parse to AST (raises SyntaxError on malformed input)
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expression, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid expression syntax: {e}") from e

    # 3. Recursively evaluate only whitelisted nodes
    def _eval(node):
        if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
            return _eval(node.body)
        if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
            if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
                return node.value
            raise ValueError("Only numeric constants allowed")
        if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
            op = _ALLOWED_OPERATORS.get(type(node.op))
            if op is None:
                raise ValueError(f"Operator not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
            left, right = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)
            # DoS guard for exponentiation
            if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_POWER_EXPONENT:
                raise ValueError("Exponent too large")
            return op(left, right)
        if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
            op = _ALLOWED_OPERATORS.get(type(node.op))
            if op is None:
                raise ValueError("Unary operator not allowed")
            return op(_eval(node.operand))
        # Anything else (Call, Attribute, Name, Subscript, Import...) is rejected
        raise ValueError(f"Disallowed expression element: {type(node).__name__}")

    return _eval(tree)
```

**Why this works:** `ast.parse` never executes code. The recursive evaluator only handles `Constant`, `BinOp`, and `UnaryOp` — so `Call`, `Attribute`, `Name`, `Import`, and `Subscript` nodes (the attack surface) are structurally impossible to reach.

---

## 3. OWASP Top 10 Mapping for Math Evaluation

| OWASP Risk | How it applies to expression evaluation | Control |
|---|---|---|
| **A03 – Injection** | `eval()`/`exec()` on user input → RCE | AST whitelist, no `eval` |
| **A04 – Insecure Design** | No threat model for untrusted input | Define trust boundary; assume all input hostile |
| **A05 – Security Misconfiguration** | `eval` with `__builtins__` tricks | Remove `eval` entirely; fail closed |
| **A06 – Vulnerable Components** | Outdated `sympy`/parser libs with CVEs | Pin & scan dependencies (`pip-audit`) |
| **A08 – Data Integrity** | Unsafe `pickle`/`yaml.load` for expression data | Use JSON; `yaml.safe_load` |
| **A09 – Logging Failures** | Silent failures hide attacks | Log rejected expressions with context |

---

## 4. Additional Hardening Layers

**Resource-exhaustion (DoS) guards** — `9**9**9` or huge factorials can hang a process:
- Cap expression length, exponent magnitude, and recursion depth.
- Run evaluation in a subprocess with a timeout and memory limit (`resource.setrlimit` on Linux) for untrusted workloads.

**If you must use `sympy`** (symbolic math), never pass raw strings to `sympify()` — it uses `eval` internally. Use `parse_expr(..., evaluate=False)` with a restricted `local_dict` and `global_dict`, or better, build expressions programmatically from validated tokens.

**Error handling** — fail closed and return generic messages to users; log details server-side:
```python
import logging
logger = logging.getLogger("math.eval")

try:
    result = safe_eval(user_input)
except ValueError as e:
    logger.warning("Rejected expression: %r — %s", user_input[:200], e)
    raise  # surface a generic error to the caller
```

**Never** use `pickle` to transport expressions or results (A08) — use JSON.

---

## 5. Security Test Cases

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr,expected", [
    ("(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1", 9.0),
    ("2 ** 10", 1024),
    ("-5 + 3", -2),
])
def test_valid(expr, expected):
    assert safe_eval(expr) == expected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("malicious", [
    "__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')",   # RCE
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",            # file access
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",  # sandbox escape
    "9 ** 9 ** 9",                           # DoS
    "x + 1",                                 # undefined name
    "a.b",                                   # attribute access
    "exec('print(1)')",                      # statement injection
])
def test_rejected(malicious):
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        safe_eval(malicious)
```

---

## 6. Prioritized Recommendations

1. **Critical** — Remove all `eval()`/`exec()` on untrusted input; replace with the AST whitelist evaluator above.
2. **High** — Add DoS guards (length, exponent, timeout, memory limits).
3. **High** — Fail closed with generic user errors + detailed server-side logging (A09).
4. **Medium** — Pin and scan dependencies with `pip-audit` / `safety` (A06).
5. **Medium** — Replace `pickle`/unsafe `yaml` with JSON / `safe_load` (A08).
6. **Low** — Add the test suite above to CI as a security regression gate.

---

### Quick reference: the tooling I used
- **`validate_syntax`** — grammar-level validation that rejects non-math characters (caught the `__import__` payload immediately).
- **`evaluate`** — safe evaluation of validated expressions.
- **`security-hardening-prompt-builder`** — generated the full OWASP/NIST assessment framework above.

All three visualizations for your Chinese regional sales dashboard are ready:

# Securing Mathematical Operations in Python

Here's a complete, practical guide based on the live tool demonstrations above and OWASP-aligned hardening guidance.

## The Core Threat: `eval()` / `exec()` = Remote Code Execution

The single biggest risk in "math evaluation" code is passing user input to `eval()`, `exec()`, or `compile()`. This is **OWASP A03: Injection** and **CWE-95 (Improper Neutralization of Directives in Dynamically Evaluated Code)**.

The demonstrations above show exactly why a **strict grammar validator** matters:

| Input | Result | Why |
|-------|--------|-----|
| `2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2` | ✅ `6.5` | Valid arithmetic |
| `10 % 3` | ✅ `1` | Modulo allowed |
| `sqrt(16)` | ✅ `4` | Whitelisted function |
| `abs(-5) + max(2, 8)` | ✅ `13` | Whitelisted functions |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ Rejected | `_` / attribute access blocked |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | ❌ Rejected | Non-whitelisted call blocked |
| `2 ** 10` | ❌ Rejected | `**` not in grammar (deliberate — prevents DoS via huge exponents) |

Notice the validator **rejects by default** (allowlist, not blocklist) — the OWASP-recommended approach.

## Recommended Architecture: AST Whitelist Evaluator

Never use `eval()`. Instead, parse to an AST and walk only explicitly permitted node types.

```python
import ast
import operator

# 1. Allowlist of permitted binary/unary operators
_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,
    ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,
    ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {
    ast.UAdd: operator.pos,
    ast.USub: operator.neg,
}
# 2. Allowlist of permitted functions (no imports, no attributes)
_FUNCS = {
    "abs": abs,
    "max": max,
    "min": min,
    "round": round,
    "sqrt": __import__("math").sqrt,
}

MAX_EXPR_LEN = 256          # bound input size
MAX_NODES = 100             # bound AST complexity (DoS guard)

def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("Expression too long or invalid type")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid syntax: {e}") from e

    if sum(1 for _ in ast.walk(tree)) > MAX_NODES:
        raise ValueError("Expression too complex")

    def _eval(node):
        # Numbers only
        if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
            if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
                return node.value
            raise ValueError("Only numeric constants allowed")
        # Binary operations
        if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BIN_OPS:
            return _BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.left), _eval(node.right))
        # Unary operations
        if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY_OPS:
            return _UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand))
        # Whitelisted function calls only
        if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
            if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _FUNCS:
                raise ValueError("Function not allowed")
            if node.keywords:
                raise ValueError("Keyword arguments not allowed")
            return _FUNCS[node.func.id](*[_eval(a) for a in node.args])
        # Everything else (Attribute, Subscript, Name, Import, Lambda...) is denied
        raise ValueError(f"Disallowed expression element: {type(node).__name__}")

    return _eval(tree.body)
```

**Why this is safe:** `ast.parse` never executes code. The walker only recurses into node types you explicitly allow. `Attribute` (`.system`), `Name` (bare variables), `Subscript`, `Lambda`, and `Import` all fall through to the deny branch.

## OWASP-Aligned Hardening Checklist

**A03 – Injection (primary)**
- ✅ Replace all `eval`/`exec`/`compile` on user input with the AST allowlist above.
- ✅ Validate against a **strict grammar** (allowlist), never a blocklist of "bad" strings.
- ✅ Reject attribute access, imports, comprehensions, and lambdas outright.

**A04 – Insecure Design / DoS**
- ✅ Cap input length (`MAX_EXPR_LEN`) and AST node count (`MAX_NODES`).
- ✅ Exclude `**` (exponentiation) or bound its operands — `9**9**9` is a CPU/memory bomb.
- ✅ Guard against division by zero and `ZeroDivisionError`.

**A05 – Security Misconfiguration**
- ✅ Run evaluation in a sandboxed/least-privilege process if exposed to untrusted users.
- ✅ Set resource limits (CPU time, memory) at the process/container level.

**A09 – Logging & Monitoring**
- ✅ Log rejected expressions (with the offending input) as security events for alerting.

**Error Handling**
- ✅ Return generic errors to users; never leak stack traces or internal paths.
- ✅ Catch `ValueError`, `SyntaxError`, `ZeroDivisionError`, `OverflowError` explicitly.

## Security Test Cases

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr,expected", [
    ("2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2", 6.5),
    ("10 % 3", 1),
    ("sqrt(16)", 4.0),
    ("abs(-5) + max(2, 8)", 13),
])
def test_valid(expr, expected):
    assert safe_eval(expr) == expected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("malicious", [
    "__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')",   # RCE
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",            # file read
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",  # sandbox escape
    "2 ** 999999999",                        # DoS
    "x + 1",                                 # undefined name
    "lambda: 1",                             # lambda
    "[i for i in range(10**9)]",             # comprehension DoS
    "1/0",                                   # division by zero
])
def test_rejected(malicious):
    with pytest.raises((ValueError, ZeroDivisionError)):
        safe_eval(malicious)
```

## Key Takeaways

1. **Never `eval()` untrusted input** — it's the #1 RCE vector in "calculator" code.
2. **Allowlist, don't blocklist** — the validator above rejects anything not explicitly permitted (as the live demos confirmed).
3. **Bound resources** — length, node count, and exponent limits prevent DoS.
4. **Defense in depth** — combine grammar validation + process sandboxing + logging.
5. **Test adversarially** — include sandbox-escape and DoS payloads in your test suite.

The `validate_syntax` and `evaluate` tools demonstrated here follow exactly this pattern: a strict grammar that accepts arithmetic and whitelisted functions while rejecting imports, attribute access, and file I/O — a good reference model for your own implementation.

> ⚠️ Note: The `batch_evaluate` tool returned an internal error during this session (`handleBatchEvaluate is not a function`), so I evaluated each expression individually instead. If you rely on that tool, it may need a fix.

## What the validation probes actually showed

I ran your expressions through the syntax validator and evaluator. Results:

| Expression | `allow_variables` | Verdict | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| `2 + 3 * 4 - (10 / 2)` | `false` | ✅ valid → evaluates to **9** | normal arithmetic |
| `(2 + 3) * (4 - 1)` | `false` | ✅ valid → evaluates to **15** | parentheses honored |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | `false` | ❌ rejected (col 1, `_`) | code-execution payload blocked |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | `false` | ❌ rejected | filesystem payload blocked |
| `2 +` | `false` | ❌ rejected | incomplete expression |
| `9 ** 9 ** 9` | `false` | ❌ rejected | `**` not in this grammar |
| `2 ** 3` | `false` | ❌ rejected | confirms no exponent operator |
| `abs(-3) + max(1, 2)` | `false` | ✅ valid | function calls *are* accepted by the grammar |
| `x + 1` | `false` | ⚠️ **valid** | **flag did not reject the identifier** |
| `x + 1` | `true` | ✅ valid | same result |

### The one finding that matters most

**`allow_variables=false` did not reject `x + 1`.** The flag is not a security boundary in this validator — it is a hint, not an enforcement. Likewise, `abs(-3) + max(1, 2)` passes syntax validation, meaning the grammar tolerates call syntax. So:

> **Never treat "syntax is valid" as "safe to evaluate."** Syntax validation answers *"is this well-formed?"* — it does not answer *"is this harmless?"* Those are different questions, and only the second one protects you.

The correct architecture is **two gates**: a cheap syntax pre-check (what I just ran) *plus* a strict AST allowlist enforced by your own code before any evaluation happens.

---

## The threat model for "math" input

`eval()` on user input is the canonical **OWASP A03:2021 – Injection** case. Even the "hardened" `eval(expr, {"__builtins__": {}})` pattern is bypassable via `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` — attribute traversal reaches `os` without ever naming `__builtins__`. Related risks:

- **A04 Insecure Design** — no resource bounds → `9**9**9`-style CPU/memory exhaustion (algorithmic complexity DoS).
- **A05 Security Misconfiguration** — verbose tracebacks leaking paths, versions, internals to the caller.
- **A06 Vulnerable Components** — unpinned expression-parsing libraries.
- **A09 Logging Failures** — no audit trail of rejected payloads, so you never see the attack.

---

## Reference implementation: AST allowlist evaluator

This is the pattern to adopt. It parses, walks the tree, and permits **only** numeric literals and a fixed operator set. Anything else — names, calls, attributes, subscripts, comprehensions, f-strings, lambdas, walrus — is rejected by default.

```python
"""safe_math.py — allowlist-based arithmetic evaluator. No eval/exec anywhere."""
from __future__ import annotations

import ast
import operator
import unicodedata
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation, localcontext

MAX_INPUT_LEN = 512          # characters
MAX_NODES     = 100          # AST node budget
MAX_DEPTH     = 20           # nesting budget
MAX_ABS       = Decimal("1e12")
PRECISION     = 28


class ExpressionRejected(ValueError):
    """Raised for any input that is not plain, bounded arithmetic."""


_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add:      operator.add,
    ast.Sub:      operator.sub,
    ast.Mult:     operator.mul,
    ast.Div:      operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv,
    ast.Mod:      operator.mod,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}


def _bounded(value: Decimal) -> Decimal:
    if not value.is_finite():
        raise ExpressionRejected("non-finite result")
    if abs(value) > MAX_ABS:
        raise ExpressionRejected("result out of permitted range")
    return value


def _eval(node: ast.AST, depth: int) -> Decimal:
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise ExpressionRejected("expression nested too deeply")

    # --- the only permitted leaf: a plain numeric literal ---
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise ExpressionRejected("only numeric literals are allowed")
        return _bounded(Decimal(str(node.value)))

    # --- binary arithmetic, operator must be on the allowlist ---
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BIN_OPS:
        left  = _eval(node.left,  depth + 1)
        right = _eval(node.right, depth + 1)
        if isinstance(node.op, (ast.Div, ast.FloorDiv, ast.Mod)) and right == 0:
            raise ExpressionRejected("division by zero")
        return _bounded(_BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](left, right))

    # --- unary sign ---
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY_OPS:
        return _bounded(_UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand, depth + 1)))

    # --- everything else is denied by default ---
    raise ExpressionRejected(f"disallowed construct: {type(node).__name__}")


def safe_eval(expression: str) -> Decimal:
    if not isinstance(expression, str):
        raise ExpressionRejected("expression must be a string")

    # Normalize first: blocks fullwidth/homoglyph digit smuggling.
    normalized = unicodedata.normalize("NFKC", expression)
    if not normalized.isascii():
        raise ExpressionRejected("non-ASCII characters are not permitted")
    if len(normalized) > MAX_INPUT_LEN:
        raise ExpressionRejected("expression too long")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(normalized, mode="eval")
    except (SyntaxError, ValueError, MemoryError, RecursionError):
        # Deliberately opaque: do not echo parser internals to the caller.
        raise ExpressionRejected("invalid expression syntax") from None

    if sum(1 for _ in ast.walk(tree)) > MAX_NODES:
        raise ExpressionRejected("expression too complex")

    with localcontext() as ctx:
        ctx.prec = PRECISION
        try:
            return _eval(tree.body, 0)
        except (InvalidOperation, OverflowError, ZeroDivisionError):
            raise ExpressionRejected("arithmetic error") from None
```

**Why each guard exists**

| Guard | Blocks |
|---|---|
| `mode="eval"` | statements, assignments, `import` |
| `ast.Constant` numeric-only | string literals, f-strings, `None`, `True`/`False` |
| operator allowlist | `**` (exponent bombs), bit-shifts, matrix `@` |
| default-deny fallthrough | `Call`, `Attribute`, `Subscript`, `Name`, `Lambda`, `NamedExpr`, comprehensions |
| `MAX_NODES` / `MAX_DEPTH` | parser and evaluator recursion DoS |
| `MAX_ABS` + `is_finite()` | `1e308 * 1e308` → `inf`, silent precision loss |
| `Decimal` + fixed precision | float rounding drift in financial math |
| NFKC + ASCII-only | Unicode digit/homoglyph bypasses |
| `from None` on re-raise | traceback leakage (A05) |

If you genuinely need exponentiation, add it explicitly with a bounded exponent — never inherit `**` from the grammar:

```python
def _pow(base: Decimal, exp: Decimal) -> Decimal:
    if exp != exp.to_integral_value() or abs(exp) > 64:
        raise ExpressionRejected("exponent must be an integer with |exp| <= 64")
    if abs(base) > 1_000_000:
        raise ExpressionRejected("base too large for exponentiation")
    return base ** int(exp)
```

---

## Defense in depth beyond the parser

1. **Process isolation for untrusted/multi-tenant input.** Even a correct allowlist shares a process with your app. Run evaluation in a subprocess with `resource.setrlimit(RLIMIT_AS, ...)` and `RLIMIT_CPU`, or in a WASM sandbox. A `SIGALRM`-based wall-clock timeout is a cheap second layer.
2. **Rate limit per principal.** Bounds the cost of any single abusive client and gives you a signal to alert on.
3. **Structured audit logging (A09).** Log `{event, principal_id, input_hash, input_truncated_128, rejection_reason, request_id}` for *every* rejection. Hash rather than store raw input — the payload may itself contain secrets or be a log-injection vector. Alert on rejection-rate spikes; that is your earliest indicator of probing.
4. **Error responses.** Return a generic `"invalid expression"` with a correlation ID. Never return the exception class, AST dump, or file path.
5. **Output handling.** If results render into HTML/JS, escape them. A numeric result is safe; an echoed error message is not.
6. **Dependency hygiene (A06).** If you use a third-party parser instead of the above, pin exact versions, run `pip-audit` in CI, and read its source — several "safe eval" packages are thin wrappers over `eval`.
7. **Ban the primitives repo-wide.** Add a lint rule (ruff `S307`, bandit `B307`) failing CI on any `eval(`, `exec(`, `compile(`, or `pickle.loads(` in application code. Policy that isn't enforced by CI decays.

---

## Security test cases to add to your suite

```python
import pytest
from safe_math import safe_eval, ExpressionRejected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr,expected", [
    ("2 + 3 * 4 - (10 / 2)", 9),
    ("(2 + 3) * (4 - 1)", 15),
    ("-5 + +3", -2),
    ("7 % 4", 3),
])
def test_accepts_arithmetic(expr, expected):
    assert safe_eval(expr) == expected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("payload", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",      # code execution
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",          # filesystem
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",  # sandbox escape
    "x + 1",                               # identifier
    "abs(-3)",                             # function call
    "2 ** 3",                              # exponent
    "'a' * 3",                             # string literal
    "f'{1}'",                              # f-string
    "[i for i in range(10)]",              # comprehension
    "lambda: 1",                           # lambda
    "2 +",                                 # malformed
    "9" * 10_000,                          # length bomb
    "(" * 500 + "1" + ")" * 500,           # depth bomb
    "１ + ２",                              # fullwidth digits
    "1e308 * 1e308",                       # overflow to inf
    "1 / 0",                               # division by zero
])
def test_rejects(payload):
    with pytest.raises(ExpressionRejected):
        safe_eval(payload)
```

Note the `x + 1` and `abs(-3)` cases: **both pass the syntax validator I ran above**, and both must fail your evaluator. That gap is exactly why the AST gate is non-negotiable.

## OWASP mapping summary

| OWASP 2021 | How this design addresses it |
|---|---|
| **A03 Injection** | No `eval`/`exec`; AST allowlist; default-deny on `Call`/`Attribute`/`Name` |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | Node/depth/length/magnitude budgets; threat model treats input as hostile |
| **A05 Misconfiguration** | Opaque errors, no traceback leakage, hardened defaults |
| **A06 Vulnerable Components** | Pinned deps, `pip-audit` in CI, stdlib-only preferred |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | Every rejection logged with hash + reason + correlation ID, alerting on spikes |
| **A10 SSRF** | N/A here — but the same allowlist principle (no outbound calls from the evaluator) applies |

**Bottom line:** the validator you have is a useful *first

## Short answer

Never let user input reach `eval()`, `exec()`, or `sympify()`. Instead run every expression through a **four-stage pipeline**: length/shape limits → strict grammar validation → AST allowlist → bounded evaluation. Below is the working design, the OWASP mapping, and the evidence from live validation runs.

---

## 1. Threat model for "math" input

| Risk | CWE | Example payload |
|---|---|---|
| Arbitrary code execution | CWE-94 / CWE-95 | `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` |
| Sandbox escape via attribute access | CWE-94 | `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` |
| Resource exhaustion / DoS | CWE-400 / CWE-1333 | `9**9**9`, `10**10**10`, huge string concat |
| Divide-by-zero / silent failure | CWE-369 | `1/0` |
| Information leak in errors | CWE-209 | full traceback returned to client |
| ReDoS in regex "sanitizers" | CWE-1333 | catastrophic backtracking patterns |

The critical insight: **a grammar check alone is not enough.** A grammar can legitimately accept `abs(-5) + 2` (verified below), so the *evaluator's namespace* is the real security boundary.

---

## 2. Live validation evidence

I ran the actual validator/evaluator against representative inputs:

| Input | Syntax verdict | Evaluation | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|---|
| `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1` | ✅ valid | `9` | Normal path works |
| `2 + 3 * 4` | ✅ valid | `14` | Precedence correct |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected — `Unexpected character '_'` | not run | Injection blocked at parse layer |
| `9**9**9` | ❌ rejected — `Expected expression` | not run | `**` unsupported → DoS vector closed |
| `2 + 3 * (4 - 1` | ❌ rejected — `Expected ')'` | not run | Unbalanced parens caught |
| `abs(-5) + 2` | ✅ **valid** | `7` | ⚠️ **Function calls are permitted** — namespace must be locked down |
| `1/0` | ✅ valid | `null` | ⚠️ **Silent failure** — error is masked, not raised |

Two findings worth acting on immediately:

- **`abs(-5) + 2` → 7** proves the grammar allows call syntax. If your evaluator resolves names against Python builtins or module globals, that is a direct RCE path. Restrict to an explicit allowlist of pure functions, or forbid `ast.Call` entirely.
- **`1/0` → `null`** means arithmetic faults are being swallowed. A `null` result that looks like a valid number type is dangerous downstream (silent data corruption, `None` propagating into pricing/limits logic). Raise a typed error instead.

---

## 3. Reference implementation (AST allowlist)

```python
import ast
import math
import operator

class ExpressionError(ValueError):
    """Raised for any rejected or failed expression."""

# --- Hard limits (tune per endpoint) ---
MAX_LEN        = 256
MAX_NODES      = 100
MAX_DEPTH      = 20
MAX_ABS_RESULT = 10**12
MAX_POW_EXP    = 64
MAX_POW_BASE   = 10**6

_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,       ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,      ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}

# Explicit allowlist of pure functions. Nothing else is reachable.
_SAFE_FUNCS = {
    "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max,
    "round": round, "sqrt": math.sqrt,
}

def _check_depth(node, depth=0):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise ExpressionError("expression nested too deeply")
    for child in ast.iter_child_nodes(node):
        _check_depth(child, depth + 1)

def _eval(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval(node.body)

    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        # Reject bool (subclass of int) and any non-numeric literal
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise ExpressionError("only numeric literals are allowed")
        return node.value

    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BIN_OPS:
        left, right = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)

        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow):
            if abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP or abs(left) > MAX_POW_BASE:
                raise ExpressionError("exponent or base out of allowed range")

        if isinstance(node.op, (ast.Div, ast.FloorDiv, ast.Mod)) and right == 0:
            raise ExpressionError("division by zero")

        result = _BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](left, right)

        if isinstance(result, complex):
            raise ExpressionError("complex results are not allowed")
        if isinstance(result, float) and not math.isfinite(result):
            raise ExpressionError("non-finite result")
        if abs(result) > MAX_ABS_RESULT:
            raise ExpressionError("result out of allowed range")
        return result

    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY_OPS:
        return _UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand))

    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _SAFE_FUNCS:
            raise ExpressionError("function not allowed")
        if node.keywords:
            raise ExpressionError("keyword arguments not allowed")
        return _SAFE_FUNCS[node.func.id](*[_eval(a) for a in node.args])

    # Everything else — Attribute, Subscript, Lambda, Comprehension,
    # NamedExpr, Starred, JoinedStr, etc. — is denied by default.
    raise ExpressionError(f"disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")

def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise ExpressionError("expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise ExpressionError("expression too long")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError:
        raise ExpressionError("invalid syntax") from None   # no traceback leak

    if sum(1 for _ in ast.walk(tree)) > MAX_NODES:
        raise ExpressionError("expression too complex")
    _check_depth(tree)

    return _eval(tree)
```

**Why each guard exists**

- `mode="eval"` — rejects statements (`import`, assignments, `;` chains) at the parser level.
- Default-deny `raise` at the end — new Python syntax can't silently become reachable.
- `ast.Constant` type check — blocks strings, bytes, `None`, and `True`/`False`.
- Pow limits — `9**9**9` is a few characters but astronomically expensive; the grammar may reject `**`, but don't rely on that.
- `math.isfinite` — catches `1e308 * 1e308 → inf` and `inf - inf → nan`.
- `raise ... from None` — prevents chained tracebacks leaking internals (CWE-209).

---

## 4. Defense in depth (beyond the parser)

1. **Process isolation.** Run evaluation in a worker (subprocess, `multiprocessing`, or a container) with `resource.setrlimit(RLIMIT_CPU, ...)` and `RLIMIT_AS`. A pure-Python loop can't be interrupted by a node budget alone if you ever add loops.
2. **Wall-clock timeout.** `signal.alarm` works only on the main thread; prefer a worker with `join(timeout)` and hard-kill on expiry.
3. **Rate limiting per identity.** Prevents brute-force probing of the grammar and CPU-burn abuse (OWASP A04/A07).
4. **Structured audit logging.** Log `{user_id, expr_hash, verdict, reason, duration_ms}` for every rejection. Never log raw secrets; hash long inputs.
5. **Precision policy.** For money, use `decimal.Decimal` or `fractions.Fraction` with explicit context — floats silently lose cents.
6. **Dependency hygiene.** `sympify()` is documented as unsafe on untrusted input (it calls `eval`). If you need SymPy, use `parse_expr` with an empty `global_dict` and `evaluate=False`, and pin versions (OWASP A06/A08).

---

## 5. OWASP Top 10 mapping

| OWASP | Applies here | Control |
|---|---|---|
| **A03 Injection** | Primary risk — `eval`/`exec`/`sympify` | AST allowlist, no `eval`, default-deny |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | No resource budget, no threat model | Node/depth/magnitude caps, worker isolation, rate limits |
| **A05 Security Misconfiguration** | Debug tracebacks, permissive CORS | Generic error messages, `raise ... from None`, prod config |
| **A06 Vulnerable Components** | Outdated SymPy/NumPy | Pin + scan (`pip-audit`, Dependabot) |
| **A08 Integrity Failures** | Unpinned deps, unsigned artifacts | Lockfiles, hashes, signed builds |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | Rejections invisible | Structured audit log + alerting on spikes |
| **A01 / A07** | Who may submit; abuse volume | AuthN/AuthZ on endpoint, per-user quotas |
